- **Device list.** `readDeviceList()` reads the session's `listDevices` (`GET /devices`): the account's devices and the end of a registration lock in force (D5b on #95), for the [device list service](#device-list-service) of the main process only; no IPC channel reaches it. It is a read, not an action of the person, with the rules of the [workspace reads](#account) below: it takes no `operation` lock and needs no cooldown, answers `unavailable` without a request while a sign-in, a sign-out, "This is not me", a removal, or an unlock runs, except during the browser part of a renewal, when it is answered (#233; the device list service still waits for the sign-in's end), waits for the startup refresh and a background refresh, and drops its answer when such an action began or the code of a renewal arrived while it was on its way, or the account or the device changed (`accountChanges`); its failures are `WORKSPACE_READ_FAILURES`, and a session that throws is reported and answers `unavailable`. The two reads share one function (`readWith`). Until #95 it was an action with its own channel, `account:list-devices`, which took the lock and had a cooldown of 2 seconds; the channel and the "Show devices" button are gone.
- **Other devices (#95, D1).** `revokeOtherDevice(device, reason)` revokes another device of the account, and `liftRegistrationLock(lockedUntil)` lifts the registration lock, for the [device list service](#device-list-service) only, which passes an active device of its list or the device of a notice, and asks for the lift only while its last list reported a lock, with its end. They are fixed functions of the session, like sign-out and removal, not entries of the write allowlist. Each is an action of the person: while a sign-in, a sign-out, a removal, "This is not me", an unlock, or another device action runs, or the key loads, it answers `refused` with `unavailable` and asks nothing; otherwise it takes the service's `operation` lock, so those answer `busy` meanwhile and reads and writes `unavailable`, and waits for a background refresh. It needs a signed-in device with a ready key (`not_signed_in` otherwise, also while signed out, after `sign_in_required`, and while a removal is pending; a device that is `signed_in` is never signed out, because both come from the same state of the session, but the key's state comes from the key store's load and is checked on its own), and a revocation refuses this installation's own device (`current_device`), which "Remove this device" removes. It then asks the person in a native dialog of the main process, and sends the session's `revokeOtherDevice` or `liftRegistrationLock` once. They do not count as changes of the account for the reads (`accountChanges`), because neither changes this installation's device or account.
  - **Reasons.** ADR 0014 names both reasons for another device: the revoke route takes `removed` or `lost` ("Revocation, sign-out, and recovery" and the routes table), removing a device is a revocation as `removed`, and a lost or stolen device is revoked as `lost` from another device of the person, which starts the 7-day registration lock. The section therefore offers "Remove" (`removed`, no lock) and "Revoke as lost" (`lost`) for each other active device; the API refuses `lost` for the calling device itself.
  - **Confirmations.** `REVOKE_DEVICE_CONFIRMATIONS` holds one warning for each reason, with the revoke button first and "Keep" as the default and the cancel button, like "Remove this device"; the message names the device's label in quotes, its platform, and the login (`revokeDeviceConfirmationMessage`), for example `Revoke the device "Work laptop" (Windows) of the GitHub account octocat as lost?`. The detail says that the server revokes the device at once, that it can no longer connect, and that its agents are disabled and their unfinished runs cancelled; for `lost` it adds that this starts a 7-day registration lock during which no new device can register unless the lock is lifted on an active device, and for `removed` that no lock starts. `LIFT_REGISTRATION_LOCK_CONFIRMATION` asks `Lift the registration lock of the GitHub account octocat, which ends at 16 Nov 2026, 10:00?` (`liftRegistrationLockConfirmationMessage`, with the end that the list reported in `dialogTimeFormat`: the date and the time as the devices section shows them, in the application's locale and the system's time zone; no earlier native dialog showed a time) and says that new devices can then register again at once, and to lift it only when the person is about to register a new device. A renderer that misbehaves cannot answer them.
  - **Results.** `revoked` with the reason, or `lifted`; `cancelled` for a kept confirmation; `unknown` when the answer does not show whether the API acted (no answer, a `5xx`, or an answer that cannot be read), which is never sent again on its own; `refused` with `unavailable`, `not_signed_in`, `current_device`, or `not_listed` (`REVOKE_DEVICE_REFUSALS`), or with `unavailable`, `not_signed_in`, or `no_lock` (`LIFT_REGISTRATION_LOCK_REFUSALS`); and `failed` with a code of `DEVICE_LIST_ACTION_FAILURES`: the codes of the other device actions without the uncertain ones, and the revoke route's `not_found` and `conflict` (`deviceListActionFailure` in [`account-session.ts`](src/main/account-session.ts)). A confirmation that cannot be shown is `failed` with `failed`; a session that throws once it was asked is `unknown`.
- **Device actions heard.** `onDeviceAction(listener)` calls its listeners after a sign-out, a removal of this device, "This is not me", a revocation of another device, or a lift of the registration lock ended with any result but a refusal before it began (`refused`) or a kept confirmation (`cancelled`), after the action released its lock, so that a read that a listener starts is not refused for the action itself. The device list service reloads then. A listener that throws is reported and stops nothing.
- **Action results.** Sign-out and removal are refused as `busy` while a sign-in is open, the key is loading, or another action runs, and as `not_signed_in` without an account or a session. A failure of the session becomes a fixed code (`DEVICE_ACTION_FAILURES`): its reasons one to one, the API codes `sign_in_required`, `auth_failed`, `device_revoked`, `device_account_mismatch`, and `rate_limited`, `refused` for another API code, and `failed` for anything else.
- **This is not me.** After a sign-in, the panel names the GitHub login that the device is signed in to and offers "This is not me" (#149). While the device is signed out, the panel names no account but still offers the button; the native dialog names the login, which the main process reads from the record (#195). The button only asks the main process, which confirms in a native dialog (`dialog.showMessageBox`, with the login, "Remove from this installation", and "Keep" as the default and the cancel button), like the folder dialog of the repositories; `cancelled` deletes nothing. It is the only confirmation: a renderer that misbehaves cannot answer it, and a second one in the page would only repeat it. The native dialog says what happens to the device at the server (#198). The main process chooses the case before it asks, from the session's `removalAsksApi`, which applies exactly the checks of `removeDevice` and of its access token (an API origin, a device that is not gone, a loaded key, no sign-out, and a cached token or a mint that the API's last refusal does not stop) and sends nothing: `server_first` when the removal would send a request to the API, and `local_only` otherwise, also without a session or when the session fails. A refused sign-in with another GitHub account can hide an earlier `sign_in_required` or `auth_failed` behind `account_mismatch`, and a WebSocket close can add one while the mismatch is shown; the refusal still stops the mint, so such a device is `local_only`. For `server_first` the dialog says that the device is first removed from that account on the server, and that it stays listed for that account, without a key, when the server cannot be reached or does not remove it. For `local_only` it says that the server cannot remove the device, so it stays listed for that account, without a key; to remove it there, the person chooses Keep, and whoever owns the account enters the passphrase of the device key or signs in with that account on this device, as the panel asks, and chooses "Remove this device". Since the devices section can remove another device of the account, it also says that whoever owns the account can remove this device from another of their devices, with "Remove" in the device list on the Account screen, either way (the note of 2026-11-06 on #95, from #198 and #205). A device forgotten on this installation only stays active at the API: nothing revokes it on its own, it counts toward the account's 20 active devices, and the agents that it hosts are not disabled ([ADR 0014](../../docs/architecture/decisions/0014-accounts-devices-and-invitations.md), update of 2026-11-06). The main process then removes the device at the API through the session's `removeDevice` where it can, and forgets the account locally whatever the API answered: it deletes the device record and the registration marker, resets the flow (`reset`), deletes the key, and refreshes the session. The API removal comes first because it needs the key, which signs the access token request, and the record, which names the device; after the local delete nothing could sign for the device. Its failure (offline, signed out, a key that waits for its passphrase, a refusal, an error) never stops the local part, so the account never stays on the installation because the API could not be reached, and the removal itself deletes files only after the API confirmed it, so it cannot bring the account back either. The result `forgotten` says with `removedAtServer` whether the API confirmed the removal, and the panel says either that the server removed the device or that it stays listed for that GitHub account, without a key, and that whoever owns that account can remove it from another of their devices, with "Remove" in the device list, as the dialog says (the Project Manager's decision of 2026-11-09). A removal that the session noted as pending ends with the local delete. The record goes first, because a record without a key would make the next sign-in name this device as its previous device. A key without a record is deleted by the next sign-in unless the [registration marker](#sign-in-flow) names it, so the marker goes right after the record: otherwise a key whose delete fails would be sent again for the forgotten account (#153). A marker that cannot be deleted is reported and does not stop the rest; once the key is gone, it names no key. The reset comes right after the record, before the key: if the key delete then fails, the flow no longer holds a record that its next attempt would write back (after a `200` whose record could not be saved), so the account cannot return. It works in every key state but `loading`, also for a key whose passphrase is not known (then local only).
- **Passphrase.** The passphrase is the only secret that crosses IPC, from the renderer to the main process, in `account:unlock-key`. It goes to the key store's `load`; the service keeps no copy, and no status, result, or message holds it. A wrong passphrase is refused as `wrong_passphrase`, and the field is cleared after every attempt. After a wrong passphrase, the next unlock waits 1 second, and each further wrong one doubles the wait up to 30 seconds (`unlockDelayMs`); an unlock during the wait, also with the right passphrase, is refused as `too_soon` without asking the key store. A success clears the count.
- **Connection.** The [connection client](#connection) of #26 uses two functions of the service, in the main process only. `signWebSocketChallenge` returns the session's `auth.response` frame, or nothing: while a key replacement is pending, it authenticates once, which resolves the replacement, and signs again. `deviceRefused(code, deviceId)` passes a close reason (`auth_failed`, `device_revoked`, or `sign_in_required`) to the session's `deviceRefused` and follows the state it answers, so that `device_revoked` stops local execution as above; when the session fails, `device_revoked` still stops it. Neither is an IPC channel, and neither is an action of the person: they take no `operation` lock and never wait for a background refresh, so a handshake never makes the person's action answer `busy`, and a revocation applies at once; the session orders its own use of the key and the files. Only the authentication for a pending replacement, which sends requests, is left out while an action, a sign-in, or a background refresh runs; the connection then backs off and tries again.
- **Workspace reads.** `readWorkspaceData(name, params, query?)` sends one of nine fixed `GET` reads of the allowlist in [`src/main/api-reads.ts`](src/main/api-reads.ts) for #27, #28, and #95, in the main process only: no IPC channel and no bridge member reach it, and chat messages are sent over the connection. The reads are the account's workspaces (`GET /workspaces`, capped at 2 MiB), a workspace view (`GET /workspaces/:workspaceId`, 1 MiB), its projects (512 KiB), a page of a project's chat history (`beforeSeq`, and `limit` from 1 to 50, always sent; 4 MiB), its agents (1 MiB), its runs (4 MiB), and, for the screens of #95 (decision D1 of 2026-11-06), a workspace's live invitations (`GET /workspaces/:workspaceId/invitations`, 512 KiB), its pending join requests and the blocks in force (`GET /workspaces/:workspaceId/join-requests`, 1 MiB), and the account's own join requests (`GET /join-requests`, 1 MiB). A `forbidden` answer, for example to a person who is neither the owner nor an admin, is `failed`. Each answer is read up to its cap and parsed with the protocol's response schema; every other route keeps the 512 KiB default, except the device list ([1 MiB](#session)). The caps hold the largest answers that the protocol's limits allow, with room to spare; the runs' cap holds about 900 finished runs, so a server that sends more than that needs a desktop with a larger cap. A name off the allowlist, or a parameter or a query outside its format, is refused as `failed` before anything is sent, also when a caller's types do not hold at runtime. A read is not an action of the person: it takes no `operation` lock and never makes an action answer `busy`. While a sign-in, a sign-out, "This is not me", a device removal, or an unlock runs, it answers `unavailable` without a request, and the caller tries again later; the browser part of a renewal is the exception ("Renewal" above, #233). During the startup and a background refresh, which may report a sign-out or retry a removal with the key, it waits for the refresh to end without the lock and then checks again, so a read right after a start is delayed rather than refused (decided on 2026-11-05). The actions do not wait for a read that is on its way. Instead, a counter (`accountChanges`) goes up when one of those actions begins and when the account (also while the status hides it), the key's state, or the device changes (except a device that becomes signed in); a read takes it before its request, and the data of its answer is dropped as `unavailable` when it changed, also when the action has ended before the answer arrives, for example a sign-out that started and finished while the read was on its way. A failure carries no data and is answered as it is, also when the read's own refusal changed the device (for example `sign_in_required` answers `not_signed_in`). An answer is also dropped when no device is signed in any more (`not_signed_in`) or another one is (`unavailable`). The failures are a closed set (`WORKSPACE_READ_FAILURES`): `not_signed_in` (also for a signed-in device whose key is not usable: it waits for its passphrase, it is lost, or it serves only a pending removal; the caller reads `status().deviceKey` to tell an unlock from a sign-in), `not_found`, `rate_limited` (the caller waits a fixed time; `Retry-After` is not read), `unavailable`, and `failed` (an answer too large or outside its schema, or another refusal). The checks that a schema cannot make, such as a page's `seq` below its `beforeSeq`, stay with the [chat timeline](#chat-timeline).
- **Workspace actions (#95).** `sendWorkspaceAction(name, params, ...body)` sends one of 24 fixed `POST` writes of a second allowlist in [`src/main/api-actions.ts`](src/main/api-actions.ts), as the Project Manager decided on 2026-11-06 (D1 on #95), in the main process only. The nine people actions reach it through their own strict channels ([People actions](#people-actions)); the other writes get theirs with their screens. Each entry is the protocol's route with its path parameters, its request schema or none, and its answer schema or `empty` for a `204`: create a workspace; define, rename, and remove a person (one route for unclaimed and claimed people; the API checks who may remove whom); promote and demote; leave; offer, accept, and withdraw ownership; create and revoke an invitation; preview and accept an invitation code; create and withdraw a join request; approve (with an optional display name), deny, deny all, block, unblock, pause, and resume join requests; and create a project (D7). "Deny and invite instead" is a deny and then a create, as ADR 0014 records for #92, so it has no entry. The limit of unclaimed people and project renames stay off the list, because no screen of #95 sends them, and the device actions become fixed functions of the session, like sign-out and removal. Every path parameter is checked as a lowercase UUIDv7 before it enters a path, and the body with the entry's request schema; a key that the schema does not know is refused, not dropped, and the body is sent as the schema parsed it, so a project's name goes normalized. The body is required for a write with one, also for an approval, whose keys are all optional (`{}`), and not allowed for a write without one (`WorkspaceActionBodyArgument`, a conditional rest tuple). A name off the list, a missing or extra parameter, or a body outside its schema answers `invalid` without a request, and so does a value that the session finds outside the protocol's format (`invalid_request`), which the allowlist's checks leave no room for. Like a read, a write takes no `operation` lock, answers `unavailable` without a request while a sign-in, a sign-out, "This is not me", a device removal, or an unlock runs (except during the browser part of a renewal, #233), and waits for the startup refresh and a background refresh. It is sent once: an uncertain answer (no answer, a `5xx`, or an answer outside its schema) answers `unknown` and is never sent again on its own, because a second invitation, for example, would revoke the first one and lose its code. The session's one retry after a `401` stays, because a `401` with the API's error body means that nothing was committed, and the retry is bound to the same account: it goes only for the device and the account of the first token, and otherwise the write answers `not_signed_in` without a second request (the Project Manager's decision of 2026-11-06). A write whose account changes while it is on its way and that succeeds still answers `unknown`, as below. Nothing is dropped silently: a failure carries no data and is answered as it is, and a success answers `unknown` when no device or another one is signed in by then, or when an action of the person began meanwhile (`accountChanges`), so that one account's answer never reaches another's screen. The failures are a closed set (`WORKSPACE_ACTION_FAILURES`): `invalid`, `too_soon`, `not_signed_in`, `unavailable`, the API's `forbidden`, `not_found`, `conflict`, `already_member`, `invitation_invalid`, `join_requests_closed`, `workspace_not_found`, `device_too_new`, `unclaimed_limit` (#242, without a wait), and `project_limit` (#255, without a wait) as they are, `rate_limited`, `unknown`, and `failed` (another refusal, such as `bad_request`). Only `unknown` leaves open whether the API acted. `rate_limited` carries `retryAfterMs` from the answer's `Retry-After`, which `callApi` now reads for `rate_limited` only, as a whole number of seconds from 1, capped at 7 days (`MAX_RETRY_AFTER_SECONDS`, the Project Manager's decision of 2026-11-06), and which the session's `request` passes on as `retryAfterSeconds`, also from a refused token request; without one, the wait is a minute (`ACTION_RATE_LIMITED_WAIT_MS`). The cap is the API's longest wait: a workspace with 50 live invitations waits until the soonest one expires, and an invitation lives up to 7 days ([`packages/database/src/invitations.ts`](../../packages/database/src/invitations.ts), `INVITATION_LIFETIME_SECONDS`); an account with 5 pending join requests waits until the soonest one ends, and a join request lives 168 hours ([`packages/database/src/join-requests.ts`](../../packages/database/src/join-requests.ts)). An installation sends at most 30 writes in any minute (`WORKSPACE_ACTION_BUDGET`), a tenth of the API's 300 requests a minute; a write over it answers `too_soon` with `retryAfterMs` until the oldest write leaves the window. A write counts once the service hands it to the session, before the request, so writes started together count at once; it also counts when the session then sends nothing (signed out, a pending removal, a key that is not loaded, no API origin, or a token that cannot be minted), because the budget also limits the token requests. Only a write that the service refuses itself (`invalid`, `unavailable`, `not_signed_in`, or `too_soon`) does not count; a session's `invalid_request`, which the service also answers as `invalid`, has counted already. **Invitation code (D2).** The code travels only in the body of a preview or an accept, as the text that the person pasted, checked against `INVITATION_CODE_PATTERN` before it is sent (exactly: white space around it is refused, and the main process trims nothing), and in the answer of a create, which goes back to the caller once. The [join screen](#join-screen) trims a pasted code and checks it with the pattern before it sends it, and its tests include a paste with a trailing newline (the Project Manager's decision of 2026-11-06). The service never keeps, logs, or publishes it, and no failure or message names it; deny-and-invite will return it the same way. **User IDs (D3).** The answers of `createWorkspace`, `acceptInvitation`, `approveJoinRequest`, and `defineMember` carry the person's `member.userId` (`WorkspaceMemberSchema`), and decision D3 on #95 keeps user IDs in the main process: the IPC pull request must not forward a `WorkspaceActionAnswer` as it is, but removes the user IDs and tests that (the Project Manager's decision of 2026-11-06). The people actions answer a definition with the new person's member ID only. The tests ([`api-actions.test.ts`](src/main/api-actions.test.ts), `account.test.ts`, `api-reads.test.ts`, `api-call.test.ts`, and `session.test.ts`) cover every entry's path, method, parameters, and schemas pinned to the protocol's; names off the list, missing, extra, and invalid parameters, bodies outside their schemas, and texts that are not invitation codes, by type and at runtime; the mapping of every session reason and every API code, with each code that a write's route answers mapped to its own failure; `Retry-After` read, capped at 7 days, and passed on, also from a token request; the body required by type exactly where a write has one; `unavailable` during each action of the person and during the startup refresh when an action starts; one request after each uncertain answer; `unknown` after a sign-out or a revocation while a write was on its way; the budget, also against writes started together; a retry after a `401` that is not sent for another device or account; the account read again after a write with a pending replacement; and that the code reaches no log, status, message, or failure.
- **Secrets.** The status and the results hold no sign-in code, verifier, signature, key, or token, and the device list no user ID either; the flow and the session never hand them over, and the contract drops unknown keys. Messages are fixed text.

Not in this part: choosing a passphrase or an unprotected key for a new Linux key without a secret service, which needs the flow's `newKeyProtection` (until then such a sign-in does not start); resetting a key whose passphrase is forgotten while the installation is signed out; and the screens of #95.

The tests ([`account.test.ts`](src/main/account.test.ts), [`account-session.test.ts`](src/main/account-session.test.ts), [`account-ipc.test.ts`](src/main/account-ipc.test.ts), [`AccountPanel.test.tsx`](src/renderer/AccountPanel.test.tsx), [`account-view.test.ts`](src/renderer/account-view.test.ts)) use fakes of the key store, the record store, the flow, the session, and the window, and cover the session's creation after the key's load (also with a passphrase), its refresh at startup and after an unlock, the report of a pending sign-out at startup, a session that cannot be created, every session state as its code without the session's account, `revoked` (local execution stopped once per change, the flow reset, the account signed out), `signInStarted` and `signInFinished` around every start and outcome, the second start after an authentication that resolved a pending replacement (also when both keys were lost), and none after one that did not, after a cancel, or for a second `replacement_pending`; sign-out and removal (confirmed, kept, pending, refused, and busy while the confirmation is open) with their failure codes and refusals; the device list read (the session's devices and lock passed on, the failure mapping, a session that throws, `unavailable` while the key loads, a sign-in runs, an action runs, or its confirmation is open, an answer dropped after a kept removal or a revocation while it was on its way, and no lock, so a sign-out runs while it is on its way); the listeners of a device action (heard after a sign-out, a refused removal, and "This is not me", not after a kept confirmation or a refusal before the action, after the lock, a listener that throws, and one removed); "This is not me" with the API removal first and the local delete whatever it answered; every action waiting for a background refresh (at startup and after an unlock) while others answer `busy`; sign-out refused while a removal is pending; removal refused before the confirmation while the key waits for its passphrase; and the startup states, the passphrase (refused, accepted, never sent back or reported, and the growing wait after wrong ones), one sign-in at a time, the cooldown between starts that opened the browser, a start in another browser (no browser opened, the URL with the warning, `busy`, `key_not_ready`, the cooldown, a waiting sign-in that must end before it, the option kept for the second start after a resolved replacement, `browser: "other"` in its status, and the panel's delay for a waiting sign-in no shorter than the cooldown), cancel, the status transitions, the outcome mapping for every listener error, API error code, and failure reason, the window coming up at the browser's post and not for a cancel or a timeout, a callback before `start` returns and a late one, the reads and writes of a renewal (#233: answered while the browser is open, `unavailable` during its start and from its code on, kept through a cancel or a timeout, paused for the whole length of any other sign-in, and the answers on their way dropped at the code, across the key replacement, and after an outcome without the code), and "This is not me" with the native confirmation (kept, failed, and busy while open), its order, the flow's reset, and its failures. The handlers refuse a foreign sender and arguments outside the contract without naming them. One test renews the sign-in of a signed-in device through the handlers with the real flow, key store, record store, session, and listener and a fake API that replaces the key, drops the tokens, and answers a new end. A first renewal that the API refuses for another GitHub account keeps the device signed in with its key and its end, names the login in the outcome, and the next read works. For the renewal that succeeds: the request is an existing device's with the same device ID and names the login, the record holds the new key and the new end, no replacement stays pending, every status keeps the device signed in, no sign-out is sent, and the next read mints a token with the new key. One test runs, through the handlers, with the real flow, key store, record store, session, and listener and a fake API with the rules of the API's device routes, a sign-in, the device list through the real device list service and its channels (refused before the sign-in, loaded when asked, dropped at the sign-out, loaded again on its own after the second sign-in, and dropped at the removal), a sign-out (the key and the record kept, and the list and removal refused without a request), a second sign-in of the same device, and the removal of this device (the key and the record deleted after the API confirmed), and searches every status and answer that reached the renderer for the sign-in codes, the verifiers, the access tokens, every signature, the public keys, and the private keys in three encodings. Another runs a new and a repeated sign-in through the handlers with the real flow, key store, record store, and listener, shows that the window came up before each request, and searches every status and answer that reached the renderer for the sign-in codes, the verifiers, the signatures, the public keys, and the private keys in three encodings. A test with the real flow, key store, record store, and session reproduces "This is not me" while the startup refresh reports a sign-out of a device with a pending replacement and the API's token answer is held back: no record of the other account is written back afterwards. A second test with the real flow shows that "This is not me" whose key delete fails, after a repeated sign-in whose record could not be saved, does not bring the account back: the next sign-in is a first sign-in without the old `login`, and no record is written. A third shows that "This is not me" deletes the registration marker of an uncertain new-device registration, so that, when the key delete fails, the next sign-in deletes that key and sends a new one (#153). For a signed-out device (#195), the service tests show that no status from the sign-out on names the login, the name, the device ID, or the outcome of the sign-in, also with a pending report; that after a restart with a fake session that starts in `starting`, no status names them, also when the first state is `key_not_loaded`, `key_lost`, or `unavailable` with `signedOut`; that a refused sign-in with another account names the login in its outcome only, and that without a sign-out it still names the account; that the outcome of a later attempt still shows; that "This is not me" confirms with the login in the main process; that a change of the signed-out flag alone, with the same device state, hides the account or names it again (#204); and that the next sign-in names the account again. The test with the real session checks every status sent after its sign-out, after a refused sign-in with another account (and a removal then refused without the confirmation), and after a restart whose key cannot be decrypted, and the session tests show `signedOut` in each state before `signed_out`, also for a marker that cannot be read, for a marker that could not be written and is kept in memory only, and for the first `key_lost` status after both keys of a pending replacement were refused twice (#204). The contract keeps nothing but the kind of a `hidden` account. The service tests also cover the sign-in expiry from the record in the status, never the session's, and a renewal of a signed-in device: it starts without a sign-out, every status keeps the device signed in with the same device ID, and the status then names the new end; a renewal refused for another GitHub account keeps the device signed in and names the login in the outcome. The panel tests render every state and click every button, the end of the sign-in as a date and a time in a given locale and time zone, "Renew the sign-in" before day 25 without a sentence, from day 25 (6 days before the end) and after the end with its fixed sentence that describes the button, disabled while busy, and none of them in any other device state, while a sign-in runs, without a ready key or a server address, or for a hidden account, and after a renewal refused for another account the outcome without a request to remove the device; and the view tests cover the window of 6 days at its edges and the start of day 25, the next change by the clock, the conditions of the renewal, and the format of the end. The view tests ([`account-view.test.ts`](src/renderer/account-view.test.ts)) cover "Use another browser": a start at once without an open sign-in, and while one waits a cancel first and the start only after it ended as `cancelled`, also when that outcome arrives before the cancel's answer, never after another outcome, a failed cancel, or the end of the status; the panel's following of the status that feeds those waits, and the waiting sign-in that the button replaces. The workspace read tests ([`api-reads.test.ts`](src/main/api-reads.test.ts) and `account.test.ts`) cover each read's path, the query only for the chat history with its encoding and limits, each cap with an answer at its size and one byte over it, the response schemas, names, parameters, and queries off the allowlist (by type and at runtime), the failure mapping for every reason of the session (a new reason does not compile until it is mapped), each read's answer schema pinned to the protocol's, an answer without a value, a read during "This is not me", a sign-out, a removal, an unlock, and a sign-in with a pending replacement, a read during the startup refresh or the refresh after an unlock that is sent after it, a sign-out or a sign-in that starts while a read waits for the startup refresh, which makes the read unavailable without a request, a device list read that runs and does not stop a read, the account and the key read again after a read with a pending replacement, a read refused by its own token request that answers `not_signed_in` although the session published the refused state meanwhile (also with the real session in `account-ipc.test.ts`), an action that runs while a read is on its way, and answers dropped after a device change or a sign-in that began meanwhile, after a sign-out, a removal that ends as pending, a sign-in attempt, a refused unlock, or a cancelled "This is not me" that began and ended while the read was on its way, and after a device that signed out without an action, but kept when the device only became signed in. The tests of the other devices (`account.test.ts`) cover the confirmation with the login, the device, and the reason before the session is asked once, both reasons and the lift, `unknown` for each uncertain answer with no second request, the mapping of the API's refusals and of every session reason to the closed set, a session that throws, `unavailable` during a sign-in, a sign-out, a removal, "This is not me", an unlock, and while the key loads, with nothing asked, `busy` for the other actions and `unavailable` for reads and a second device action while a revocation's confirmation is open, a kept or failed confirmation, this installation's device, a device that is not signed in (signed out, `sign_in_required`, a pending removal, a key that waits for its passphrase, and a key that could not be loaded while the session names the device signed in), the listeners of a device action, and the dialog texts, with the end of the lock in a given locale and time zone; one test with the real session in [`account-ipc.test.ts`](src/main/account-ipc.test.ts) revokes another device as lost and lifts the lock through the device channels, with this device and an unlisted ID refused before anything is asked, the lift refused before a lock is in force, the advice after the revocation as lost and its dismissal, each lift confirmed with the lock's end, the list loaded again after each action, and an uncertain lift sent once.

## Session

The device session ([`src/main/session/`](src/main/session/)) is part 3 of #94: access tokens, the pending key replacement, the device states, sign-out, removing this device, the device list, revoking another device and lifting the registration lock (#95), and the signature of the WebSocket challenge, in the main process ([ADR 0014](../../docs/architecture/decisions/0014-accounts-devices-and-invitations.md), "Device key and authentication", "Revocation, sign-out, and recovery", and "Storage and transport on the device"; P-56, P-65). The [account service](#account) creates it after the key's load, refreshes it at startup, tells it about every sign-in, and passes its state, sign-out, and removal on to the IPC and the panel, and its device list on to the [device list service](#device-list-service); the WebSocket client of #26 uses its signature. These rules are part of the authentication contract and are reviewed with the desktop owner when they start work (P-67).

- **Access tokens.** `createDeviceSession(dependencies)` ([`session.ts`](src/main/session/session.ts)) mints a token with `POST /auth/challenge` and `POST /auth/access-token`. The device key signs the `http` input: the API origin as the audience, the nonce, the device ID from the record, and the challenge ID. The token stays in the session's memory, and no function returns it. It is used until one minute before its 15 minutes end, counted from before the request that minted it on both the device's monotonic clock and its wall clock: the monotonic clock may stop while the computer sleeps, and the wall clock may jump. A difference between the clocks of the device and the API does not matter; `expiresAt` is not read. One mint runs at a time, and concurrent callers share it. `request` sends a request with the token. A `401` drops the token, mints one new token, and sends the request once more; when the new token cannot be minted, the failure names the API's reason (`auth_failed`, `device_revoked`, or `sign_in_required`), and a second `401` answers `unauthorized`. A removal noted while the first request was on its way stops that retry before the new token (`removal_pending`). The retry goes only for the device and the account of the first token: each token carries the device ID and the account ID of the record that it was minted for, and when the new token acts for another device or account, for example after "This is not me" and a sign-in with another GitHub account while the first request was on its way, the request answers `unauthorized` without a second request (the Project Manager's decision of 2026-11-06 on #95). A `rate_limited` refusal, of the request or of its token request, carries the answer's `Retry-After` as `retryAfterSeconds`. A token request whose answer is `auth_failed` or `sign_in_required` after the key store changed meanwhile applies nothing and answers `no_device`, as a token then writes nothing: a renewal may replace the key while a read's token request signed with the previous key is on its way (#233), and a late refusal of that key must not leave the renewed device refused.
- **Requests.** [`api-call.ts`](src/main/session/api-call.ts) sends every request with the injected `fetch`, `redirect: "error"`, `cache: "no-store"`, and `credentials: "omit"`, only to a URL whose origin is the API origin (else nothing is sent, and the token never leaves for another host), stops waiting after 30 seconds, and reads at most 512 KiB of the answer by default; a request may name its own cap (`maxAnswerBytes`), as the [workspace reads](#account) and the device list do. The device list (`GET /devices`) is read up to 1 MiB (`DEVICE_LIST_MAX_ANSWER_BYTES`, #224): the protocol's largest list, 1,000 devices with every field at its longest and 64 four-byte characters in each label, is about 555 KB, so the cap holds it with room to spare, up to about 1,889 such devices. The session's `request` may also take a `query`, encoded with `URLSearchParams` after the filled path, which stays on the API origin. Only a `4xx` whose body passes `ApiErrorBodySchema` with the same status is a certain answer. A network error, the timeout, a `5xx`, and an answer that is not the API's are uncertain and change nothing on the device.
- **Pending replacement.** While the record names the previous key, the session tries the new key first. A token proves that the API holds the key that signed, and so do `sign_in_required` and `device_revoked`, which the API answers only after it verified the signature. If that is the new key, the record is written with the new public key (a second time if the first write fails), and only then is the replacement confirmed, the rule of the sign-in flow; when the record cannot be written, the replacement stays pending (`record_not_saved`). If the new key gets `auth_failed`, the previous key is tried with a new challenge, and if it authenticates, the replacement is abandoned, except during the grace period of an uncertain sign-in (below). If both get `auth_failed`, one round proves nothing: the API answers `auth_failed` also for a challenge that it lost (challenges live in its memory, so a deploy loses them) and to a resent copy of an access token request that it already answered. So the session notes the time, keeps both keys, answers `auth_failed`, and asks nothing more on its own for 10 minutes (`LOST_KEY_WAIT_MS`); `refresh` lets the person retry earlier, and a round in that time deletes nothing. The time is noted in the session notes, so a restart does not start the count again: a person whose device does not work is likely to restart the application, and the lost-key path must still be reachable. A token or `sign_in_required` for either key in between proves that the API holds a key, so it ends the first refusal, also during the grace period below; and a first refusal older than a day (`KEYS_REFUSED_MAX_AGE_MS`) says nothing about the API now, so the next refusal starts the wait again. Only a round at least 10 minutes, and less than a day, after the first one that refuses both keys again, the new key first, makes the installation treat them as lost (decided on #94): it publishes `key_lost` first, deletes both keys through the key store, and keeps the record, so the next sign-in takes the flow's lost-key path and registers a new device that names the record's device as `previousDeviceId`. A sign-out marker stays, also one that is not corrupt, so that `key_lost` still carries `signedOut` and the account stays hidden (#204): the read after the next sign-in drops a marker that names no key that the installation holds, the sign-in flow never reads it, and the read after "This is not me" deleted the record drops it with the record. An uncertain answer in any round, or a refused challenge such as `rate_limited`, changes nothing, and no further key is tried after it; `sign_in_required` and `device_revoked` keep their meaning in every round. While a sign-in runs (from `signInStarted` until `signInFinished`), a pending replacement is not resolved (`sign_in_running`), because the sign-in may still register the new key. A record that already names the new key confirms the replacement when it is read, as in the sign-in flow.
- **Grace period of an uncertain sign-in.** The API can commit a `POST /auth/sign-in` well after the flow stopped waiting (its statement timeout applies to each statement). So after every outcome of the sign-in flow other than `signed_in` and `refused` (for example `no_answer`, `server_error`, `uncertain_refusal` with any of its codes, `record_not_saved`, or `cancelled`), a pending replacement gets a grace period of 10 minutes (`REPLACEMENT_GRACE_MS`) from that outcome. During it, a token of the previous key is used, but both keys stay, and every mint tries the new key first; a late commit of the new key is then confirmed as usual. Only after it does the previous key abandon the replacement. The time is in the session notes, so a restart keeps the grace period. A pending replacement that the session sees without a noted outcome, for example after a stop during the request, gets its grace period from then; a later outcome of an attempt that sent nothing keeps the first time. So for up to 10 minutes after an uncertain sign-in, unless the new key authenticates first, a new sign-in answers `replacement_pending` (the flow does not replace a key while one is pending), and the WebSocket challenge is not signed (`replacement_pending`); HTTP requests work with the previous key's token. `replacementGraceEnd()` answers when the grace period ends, in UTC, while it protects the new key of a pending replacement, and null otherwise (#199). It is null also while a first refusal of both keys is noted, because the replacement then stays pending until the wait for a second round ends, which can be later. It counts from the smaller age of the two clocks, as the grace period does, so a wall clock that jumps forward moves the end with it, and it rounds a fraction of a millisecond up, so the end is never early. It sends no request, and like every read of the device it only tidies the session notes.
- **Device states.** `state()` and `onChange(listener)` give the account service a small state: `starting`, `no_device`, `key_lost`, `unavailable`, `key_not_loaded`, `signed_in`, `signed_out` (with `reportPending`), `sign_in_required`, `auth_failed`, `account_mismatch`, `removal_pending`, `removed`, or `revoked`, with the account from the record where there is one (device ID, user ID, login, display name, and sign-in expiry, no key). `key_lost`, `unavailable`, `key_not_loaded`, and `account_mismatch` come before `signed_out`; while a sign-out marker exists they carry `signedOut: true`, so that the account service still knows that the person signed out (#195). Without the keys, a marker that exists counts, also one that cannot be read. `key_lost` is a record without its device's key: the key file is gone, the loaded key is not the record's device's key, or both keys of a pending replacement were refused twice, 10 minutes apart; it follows from the files, so it holds after a restart too. `removal_pending` follows from the session notes, so it holds after a restart too. After `device_revoked` the session publishes `revoked` first, so that the caller stops local execution, then deletes the key, then the record (the record stays when the key could not be deleted), then the sign-out marker. `sign_in_required` and `auth_failed` keep the key and the record; the session then asks the API nothing until `refresh` or a sign-in, except to resolve a pending replacement, which the next sign-in needs. `signInFinished` with `refused` and `device_account_mismatch`, or that code in any answer of the API, moves to `account_mismatch` with the login from the record, and a failure carries the login as `deviceLogin`. A device that was `signed_in` when the sign-in started (`signInStarted` notes it) stays `signed_in` after such a refusal of the sign-in, because the API committed nothing and the flow kept its key: for example a renewal in a browser that holds another GitHub account (the Project Manager's decision of 2026-11-09 on #95). The note ends with `signInFinished`, and the account service runs one sign-in at a time. The device itself is unchanged, so the person can sign in as that login and then remove the device. `deviceRefused(code, deviceId)` applies a close reason of the WebSocket the same way, for the device that the connection authenticated as; a refusal for another device, for example a late `device_revoked` from a connection of a device that this installation no longer holds, is ignored.
- **Sign-out (P-65).** `signOut` drops the tokens and writes the sign-out marker before it calls `POST /auth/sign-out`, with the cached token while it is still valid, or else with a new token that is used for nothing else. The device, its key, and its record stay. From then on `request`, `removeDevice`, and `signWebSocketChallenge` answer `signed_out` without asking the API, also after a restart, and so does `authenticate`, except that it still resolves a pending replacement, which the next sign-in needs (see the marker below). While offline, `signOut` resolves with `pending`, the state shows `reportPending`, and `reportSignOut` reports it later. `sign_in_required` in answer to the report counts as reported, because the API then takes the key only after a new sign-in. A `401` to the report gets one new token.
- **Sign-out marker.** `device-sign-out.json` ([`sign-out-marker.ts`](src/main/session/sign-out-marker.ts)) sits next to the record and the key files (on Windows `%LOCALAPPDATA%\MyManDev`) and is written with the key store's atomic write: `{ version: 1, publicKeys, reported }`. It is a file, not memory, because the sign-out must survive a restart, which is neither a GitHub sign-in nor a report. It names the public keys that the device held when the person signed out. Every GitHub sign-in stores a new key, so a key that the marker does not name was made by a sign-in after the sign-out: the marker is deleted by a read that finds none of its keys held, and when a key that it does not name authenticates. `signInFinished` with `signed_in` relies on that rule too, so a sign-out made while that sign-in ran, which names the sign-in's new key, holds; it deletes only a corrupt marker. Any other outcome of the sign-in flow leaves it, `failed` with `uncertain_refusal`, `key_changed`, or `record_not_saved` included: after an uncertain answer the replacement stays pending, the marker still names the previous key, and only an authentication with the new key proves that the sign-in committed. A corrupt or unreadable marker names every key (fail closed) until a sign-in. When the file cannot be written, the sign-out holds in memory until the application quits, and `onError` says so. While signed out, the key signs only to report the sign-out and to resolve a pending replacement that the next sign-in needs; a token minted with a key that the marker names is used only for the report.
- **Remove this device.** `removeDevice` first notes the removal in the session notes, then revokes the device with `POST /devices/:deviceId/revoke` and the reason `removed`. Only after the API confirmed it are the key, the record, the marker, and the notes deleted, and the state is `removed`. Without a clear answer it resolves with `pending`, and the key stays. While the removal is noted, also after a restart, the state is `removal_pending`, and the key serves only the removal: `request`, `listDevices`, `authenticate`, and `signWebSocketChallenge` answer `removal_pending` without asking the API (ADR 0014 shows an offline removal as pending). `refresh`, which the application calls at start, and another `removeDevice` retry it. A refusal of the revoke itself, for example `conflict`, ends the pending removal and keeps the key; a refusal of the token (`auth_failed`, `sign_in_required`, `rate_limited`) leaves it pending. A sign-out or a sign-in ends a pending removal, because the person chose that instead. It is refused while signed out, because the API takes no request of a signed-out device: the person signs in first.
- **Session notes.** `device-session.json` ([`session-notes.ts`](src/main/session/session-notes.ts)) sits next to the sign-out marker and is written the same way: `{ version: 1, removal, uncertainSignIn, keysRefused }`, each null or naming the device ID or the public keys that it is about, with a UTC time for the last two. One small strict file holds the three, because each must survive a restart, the record belongs to the sign-in flow, and the marker has its own meaning; it holds no secret. The session reads it once (this instance holds the single-instance lock, so only it writes the file), keeps only the notes about the device and the keys that the installation holds, and deletes the file when every note is empty. The times use the wall clock, the only clock that survives a restart. A time later than now, for example after the clock was set back, counts as now and is rewritten, so a note can never wait for a date in the future. While the application runs, a wait also needs the monotonic clock to have advanced, so a wall clock that jumps forward ends no wait early; a clock that moves back, or a computer that sleeps, only makes a wait longer. The one-day limit of a first refusal uses the larger age instead: a first refusal is too old as soon as either clock says so, so days that the computer slept through count, also while the application runs. A corrupt file is reported and not used; it then loses a pending removal, a grace period, and the first refusal time, which only makes the person retry the removal or wait again.
- **Device list.** `listDevices` calls `GET /devices` and returns the answer parsed with `DeviceListResponseSchema`, frozen: the devices and `registrationLockedUntil`, the end of a registration lock in force or null (D5b on #95).
- **Other devices (#95, D1).** `revokeOtherDevice(deviceId, reason)` calls `POST /devices/:deviceId/revoke` with `{ reason }`, `removed` or `lost`, checked with the protocol's `RevokeDeviceRequestSchema`, and `liftRegistrationLock()` calls `POST /account/registration-lock/lift` without a body; both expect a `204`. Both go through `request`, so they get its one retry after a `401`, bound to the device and the account of the first token, and they are refused while a removal of this device is pending (`removal_pending`) or the device is signed out, without a request. The device of this installation's record, read in the key queue, is refused as `invalid_request` without a request, because a revocation there would leave its key and record behind; `removeDevice` removes it. An uncertain answer is returned as it is (`no_answer`, `server_error`, or `unexpected_response`) and never sent again.
- **Late answers.** An answer of the API can arrive long after the session read the files, for example while "This is not me" deletes the record and the key. Before a token or `sign_in_required` changes anything, the session reads the record again; when it is gone, names another device, or the key store no longer holds the keys that signed, it writes, confirms, and abandons nothing and answers `no_device`, so a late answer never writes an account back. `removeDevice` notes the removal inside the key queue, after a mint or a sign-out report that runs, and sends the revoke outside it.
- **WebSocket.** `signWebSocketChallenge(challenge)` returns the `auth.response` frame for the WebSocket client of #26: the `ws` input with the nonce and the connection ID of `auth.challenge`, signed with the current key ([`websocket-auth.ts`](src/main/session/websocket-auth.ts)). It sends no request, so it fits into the 10-second handshake. It is refused while signed out, while a removal is pending, after `sign_in_required` or `auth_failed`, and while a replacement is pending (`replacement_pending`); `authenticate` resolves the replacement first. The frame is the only value of the session that carries a signature.
- **Secrets.** No result, state, or message holds a token, a signature (except that frame), or a key. The optional `onError` receives fixed messages only, and the session never logs.
- **Electron.** [`session/system.ts`](src/main/session/system.ts) passes `net.fetch`, which follows the system proxy settings, `app.isPackaged`, `process.env`, the key folder, the platform, and the record store that the account service and the flow use. It stays thin and is only type-checked.
- **Sign-in expiry.** The session's account carries the record's `signInExpiresAt`, which only a sign-in writes. When the session confirms a pending replacement after an uncertain sign-in that the API committed late, it writes the record with the new key and keeps its end, so the account status can show an end before the API's, and ask to renew too early, but never too late. `auth.ok` carries the API's end, but the connection client does not pass it on. The later plan is to reconcile this device's end with the device list, which carries each device's end, and show the later of the two.
- **Known limits.** A corrupt sign-out marker names every key, so after a sign-in that committed while it was corrupt the device stays signed out until the next `signed_in` outcome deletes it (fail closed). A sign-out needs the key to be loaded (`key_not_loaded` otherwise), because the marker names the public keys that it holds. A corrupt session notes file loses a pending removal: no key is deleted, the device simply stays, and the person can request the removal again.

The tests ([`session.test.ts`](src/main/session/session.test.ts), [`session-notes.test.ts`](src/main/session/session-notes.test.ts), [`sign-out-marker.test.ts`](src/main/session/sign-out-marker.test.ts), [`api-call.test.ts`](src/main/session/api-call.test.ts), [`websocket-auth.test.ts`](src/main/session/websocket-auth.test.ts)) use a temporary folder, the real key store with a fake `safeStorage`, the real record and marker stores, injected timers, an injected monotonic clock and wall clock, and a fake API with the rules of the API's device routes: a single-use challenge, the signature checked before the device state, every signature verified with `node:crypto`, and tokens that a sign-out or a revocation ends. They cover minting and its signed input, the cache until shortly before the end, one mint for concurrent callers and for concurrent `401`s, the retry after a `401` and its limit, a retry that is not sent when the new token acts for another device or account, for a write and for a read, and the reason of the new token's failure, with the `Retry-After` of a `rate_limited` token request. For the pending replacement: the new key confirmed after the record write, in this order; a failed record write that keeps it pending; the previous key that abandons it; both keys refused, which only notes the time and asks nothing more on its own, a retry within the wait that deletes nothing, and a key that authenticates in between, which ends the first refusal (also during the grace period), a first refusal older than a day, which starts the wait again, a round 10 minutes later that refuses both again, which deletes both keys and keeps the record, after which the real sign-in flow registers a new device with `previousDeviceId`; the count across a restart; a refused round followed by the new key or the previous key in a later round, which deletes nothing; the grace period of an uncertain sign-in (the previous key's token with both keys kept, a late commit of the new key confirmed, the abandon after it, across a restart, each outcome that starts it, a later outcome that keeps its time, and a pending replacement seen after a stop); the sign-out marker after a lost key, which stays, also when it is not corrupt, so that `key_lost` carries `signedOut` from its first status on and after a restart, and which ends with the next sign-in or with "This is not me" (#204); a network error, the timeout, a `5xx`, a `503`, an answer that is not the API's, an error body whose status does not fit, a `200` that is not a token, a body over the limit, and `rate_limited`, which change nothing, for the new and for the previous key and in either round; `sign_in_required` for either key; and no resolution while a sign-in runs. For the states: `device_revoked` (the state first, then the key, then the record, also during a replacement, and a key that cannot be deleted keeps the record), `sign_in_required`, `auth_failed`, `device_account_mismatch` (a device that was signed in when the sign-in started stays signed in, with its key, a token, and the WebSocket's signature; one that was refused or signed out before, and one whose earlier sign-in ended, moves to `account_mismatch`), and close reasons of the WebSocket. For sign-out: the report with the valid token, the key kept and unused, offline and after a restart, the later report, `sign_in_required` as reported, a `401` to the report, a sign-in that ends it with and without `signInFinished`, a sign-in after the sign-out whose answer was lost (both outcomes), a corrupt marker, and a marker that cannot be written. For late answers: a token answer after the record and the keys were deleted, or for a record of another device, which writes nothing, and a removal that waits for a mint that runs. For removal: the files deleted only after the API confirmed, pending without a clear answer, a refusal, `device_revoked`, an offline removal that blocks every other use of the key, holds across a restart, and is retried by `refresh`, a removal noted while a request is on its way that stops the request's retry after a `401`, and a sign-out or a sign-in that ends it. Also: note times a year in the future, which count from now and are rewritten, a wall clock that jumps forward, which ends neither wait, and a first refusal that the computer slept through for days, which is too old; a token that ends by the wall clock, a refusal for another device that is ignored, and a sign-out made during a sign-in that holds after it, and a late `auth_failed` or `sign_in_required` of a key that the store no longer holds, which applies nothing: after a renewal replaced it, after "This is not me" deleted the keys, and after a pending replacement was confirmed meanwhile, while a late `device_revoked` still revokes the device and a late `rate_limited` keeps its wait (#233). They also cover the device list schema with the end of the registration lock and the frozen answer, the protocol's largest device list, which is over the default cap, read within the list's own cap, and an answer one byte over that cap refused, the revocation of another device with each reason and the lift (one request each with the access token, this device kept), this installation's device, another reason, and an ID outside the format refused without a request, the retry after a `401` and its binding to the first token's device and account, each uncertain answer sent once, the API's refusals, and nothing sent while a removal is pending or after a sign-out, the WebSocket frame and its signature, the marker and notes formats, the answer limit and a request's own cap at its exact size, the query's encoding, the timeout, the origin check, and the fetch options of a request, and that no token, signature, or private key reaches a result, a state, a message, or the marker file, and that no file of the session logs.

## Connection

The connection client ([`src/main/connection/`](src/main/connection/)) keeps the main process's connection to the cloud API's gateway (M2-02, #26; [ADR 0014](../../docs/architecture/decisions/0014-accounts-devices-and-invitations.md), "Device key and authentication" and "Storage and transport on the device"; P-56). The Project Manager confirmed its scope and its client on 2026-11-02. It authenticates with the [device session](#session) through the [account service](#account), and the renderer sees only its status.

- **Client.** Electron's `net.WebSocket` in the main process, in Electron 45.0.0 (added in electron/electron#51593 and released in 42.7.0, 43.2.0, and 44.0.0), with the default session, `useSessionCookies: false`, and the `origin` option unset. It adds no dependency. It runs on Chromium's network stack, so it uses the same proxy resolution (system settings, PAC, and WPAD), proxy authentication cache, and TLS trust as the `net.fetch` calls of the session. It sends `Origin` with the http(s) form of the socket URL's origin, which is the API's own origin and which the API's `/ws` accepts since #154. The gateway path `/ws` is a desktop constant (`GATEWAY_PATH`, decided on #26). [`client.ts`](src/main/connection/client.ts) takes an injected socket factory, so the tests use a fake; [`system.ts`](src/main/connection/system.ts) passes `net.WebSocket` and the `powerMonitor` `resume` event, stays thin, and is only type-checked.
- **Options considered.**

  | Option                                                     | Why not                                                                                                                                                                            |
  | ---------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
  | Electron `net.WebSocket` (chosen)                          |                                                                                                                                                                                    |
  | Node's built-in `WebSocket`                                | No proxy support, and only the bundled root certificates, so a company root for TLS inspection is not trusted.                                                                     |
  | `undici` with a proxy agent, fed by `session.resolveProxy` | Resolves the proxy only indirectly (our own parsing of the PAC result, no SOCKS4), knows Basic proxy authentication only, and needs the trust store passed by hand.                |
  | `ws` with proxy agents, fed by `session.resolveProxy`      | The same as `undici`, and three to seven new packages.                                                                                                                             |
  | Chromium's WebSocket in a hidden window                    | The connection would leave the main process (P-56), the API refuses the renderer's origin, and the signature would have to cross IPC. A utility process has no Chromium WebSocket. |

- **Start and stop.** The client follows the account status: it connects while the device state is `signed_in`, the device key is `ready`, the installation holds an account, and the API origin is set. It closes with 1000 when that ends, on sign-out, removal, and revocation, and at quit (`will-quit`). Another device ID closes the socket first. There are never two sockets: after the client closed a socket, the next one opens only after its close event, or after 5 seconds without one (`CLOSE_WAIT_MS`).
- **Authentication.** The client waits for `auth.challenge` and sends exactly one `auth.response`, the frame that the account service's `signWebSocketChallenge` returns from the session; while a key replacement is pending, the service authenticates once first, which resolves it, and signs again. It then waits for `auth.ok` of the device that signed. The opening handshake may take 15 seconds (`HANDSHAKE_TIMEOUT_MS`), and the open socket gets 15 more until `auth.ok` (`AUTH_OK_TIMEOUT_MS`); on expiry the client closes with 1000 and backs off (`timeout`). A challenge with another protocol version stops the client with `update_required`; so does any first message whose `protocolVersion` differs, even one that is not a valid challenge, because the version is read before the type. A challenge that the session does not sign closes and backs off (`signature_failed`).
- **Messages.** The client accepts only text frames of at most 1 MiB in UTF-8 (`RECEIVE_LIMIT_BYTES`, decided on #26), checked after receipt, because `net.WebSocket` has no limit of its own; `MAX_FRAME_BYTES` (64 KiB) is the server's limit for the client's frames. Each message is parsed as JSON and decoded with `decodeServerMessage`. Before `auth.ok`, only the challenge and then `auth.ok` are accepted. A binary frame, invalid JSON, a decode failure, a message over the limit, or a frame out of order closes with 1000 and backs off (`invalid_message`). After `auth.ok`, decoded messages go to the `onMessage` listeners of the main process; the renderer never gets a frame. The client sends only after `auth.ok`, and only client events, through `sendAs` (see "Identity"), and the workspace frames, each checked with `decodeClientMessage` first; only the decoded copy is sent. A send is refused above `MAX_FRAME_BYTES` (`too_large`) and while more than 1 MiB waits in `bufferedAmount` (`backpressure`, `MAX_BUFFERED_SEND_BYTES`). `subscribeWorkspace` and `unsubscribeWorkspace` keep the set of requested workspace IDs, and every `auth.ok` sends `workspace.subscribe` for each of them again. The server's answers reach the listeners; the set changes only when main-process code asks.
- **Identity.** The client keeps the `userId` and `deviceId` of the current socket's `auth.ok`, the identity that the server binds every event of the connection to (ADR 0006, identity binding). It is cleared when the socket closes, when the client closes or replaces it, and at quit, and the next `auth.ok` sets it again. `sendAs(build)` calls `build` with that identity and sends the event on that socket in the same call, so that no await, and no other socket, comes between reading the identity and sending (decided on #27 and #28 on 2026-11-05). It is the only way to send an event, as decided on the same day; the client has no `send`. Without an authenticated socket it answers `not_connected` and does not call `build`; a `build` that throws gives `invalid`, and one that closed the socket gives `not_connected`. The send guards above apply, and an event whose `deviceId`, or whose `user` actor's `userId`, is not the identity's is refused as `invalid`, because the server would close the connection with 1008 `identity_mismatch`. The identity stays in the main process and in no status or log. `identity()` returns it, or null without an authenticated socket, for main-process code that keys its data by the account and the device: the [chat service](#chat-service) keys its waiting messages with it (decision 4 of 2026-11-05 on #27 and #28 keeps such a getter for the main process only). The chat service and the [development agent switch](#development-agent-switch) call `sendAs`.
- **Close reasons** (ADR 0014's table, the protocol package's `WS_CLOSE_CODES`, and the decisions on #26):

  | Code                                       | The client                                                                                                                                      |
  | ------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
  | 4000 `auth_failed`                         | Stops (`auth_failed`). The session's `deviceRefused` notes the refusal, and the account panel offers a sign-in.                                 |
  | 4002 `device_revoked`                      | Stops (`device_revoked`). `deviceRefused` deletes the key, and the account service calls its stop-local-execution hook.                         |
  | 4003 `sign_in_required`                    | Stops (`sign_in_required`). `deviceRefused` notes it; the browser opens only when the person clicks "Sign in with GitHub" in the account panel. |
  | 4004 `connection_replaced`                 | Stops (`connection_replaced`). The header offers "Reconnect here once" and "Remove this device and register again".                             |
  | 4005 `protocol_violation`                  | Stops (`protocol_violation`) and offers "Retry now".                                                                                            |
  | 4006 `key_replaced`                        | Reconnects at once, one time; when it repeats before the connection was stable, it backs off (`key_replaced`).                                  |
  | 1008 `unsupported_version`                 | Stops (`update_required`).                                                                                                                      |
  | 4001 `auth_timeout`                        | Backs off (`timeout`).                                                                                                                          |
  | 1011, 1013                                 | Backs off (`server_error`, `server_busy`).                                                                                                      |
  | 1003, 1007, 1008 with another reason, 1009 | Backs off (`message_refused`).                                                                                                                  |
  | 1001, 1006, any other code                 | Backs off (`connection_lost`, or `connection_failed` before the socket opened).                                                                 |

  The device ID of `deviceRefused` is the one of the signed `auth.response`, so that a late close of another device's connection changes nothing. After a stop, the client waits until the device is signed in again, for example after a new sign-in; after `connection_replaced` and `protocol_violation`, the person can also reconnect (and after `connection_replaced` remove the device). Those two hold for the connection of the signed-in device only: when no device with a loaded key is signed in any more (a sign-out, "This is not me", a removal, also one that waits for the API), the status becomes the one of no device, `offline` with `not_signed_in`, because a reconnect would be refused. The client remembers that stop with its device (#217, the Project Manager's decision of 2026-11-09): when the same device is signed in again without a new sign-in, the stop comes back with its sentence and its actions, and no socket opens. That happens, for example, after "Remove this device and register again" when the API refuses the revoke with a code that ends the removal, such as `conflict`: the session ends the removal, and the device is signed in again with the same ID, although the person never chose "Reconnect here once". A new sign-in of the device ends either stop, also while the device stays signed in: it replaces the key (ADR 0014), so a copy that another installation used stops working. The account status's sign-in state tells a new sign-in. The status names no sign-in time, and a sign-in of the same device keeps its device ID and its record (P-65); its new key does not show in the status, and `replacementPending` can predate the stop. Every sign-in shows `starting` first, which clears the last outcome, and the client counts a sign-in that it saw run since the stop began, also one that already ran when the stop came: it ends a stop that holds once it ended with `signed_in`, and when the device returns, it counts while it still runs, since the session reports the device signed in before the sign-in ends. A sign-in that was cancelled or failed does not count, and neither does a `signed_in` outcome from before the stop. The memory ends when that device returns, with or without the stop, and when another device is signed in. The refusals (`auth_failed`, `device_revoked`, `sign_in_required`) stay until a new sign-in, also across a sign-out: the session moves the device to the refused state right after them, and their sentence already asks for a GitHub sign-in. `update_required` holds until the application is updated, across a sign-out and a new sign-in.

- **Backoff.** The client waits a random time between half of min(60 s, 1 s x 2^n) and all of it, where n counts the attempts; the count starts again after 60 seconds connected (`STABLE_CONNECTION_MS`). That stays well below the API's 60 handshakes per address in 10 minutes. When the system resumes (`powerMonitor` `resume`), a waiting connection starts at once, and an open one is replaced, because it may have died unnoticed during the sleep.
- **Status.** `{ state, reason?, retryAt?, revision }` (`ConnectionStatusSchema`): `offline` (with `not_signed_in` or `no_api_origin`, or without a reason while the session starts and at quit), `connecting`, `authenticating`, `connected`, `waiting` with a reason and `retryAt`, or `stopped` with a reason. `connection:reconnect` connects at once ("Retry now" while waiting, "Reconnect here once" after `connection_replaced`, and "Retry now" after `protocol_violation`). It is refused as `not_signed_in` without a signed-in device and after `auth_failed`, `device_revoked`, and `sign_in_required`, because the server would refuse the same key again and only a GitHub sign-in ends them; as `update_required`; as `already_open` while a socket is open or opening; and as `too_soon` within 3 seconds of the last reconnect (`RECONNECT_COOLDOWN_MS`) or after 10 reconnects within 10 minutes (`RECONNECT_BUDGET`). The backoff makes at most 25 handshakes in 10 minutes, when every wait is the shortest, so even a renderer that calls the reconnect in a loop keeps the client at 35, below the API's 60 per address. The shell header shows the state, a fixed sentence about the reason, and the actions ([`src/renderer/connection-status.ts`](src/renderer/connection-status.ts)). The header follows the account status as well. `offline` with `not_signed_in` says "Sign in with GitHub on the Account screen to connect." only while the account panel offers a sign-in that can start now (`canStartSignIn`); otherwise, for example while a sign-in runs, a removal waits for the server, or the device key is not ready, it says "This device connects once it is signed in and ready.", and so it does while the removal's message below asks for the sign-in already. After `connection_replaced` it offers "Reconnect here once" and "Remove this device and register again" (ADR 0014, #95), only while the account status shows this device signed in (`deviceSignedIn`); without the account bridge, before its first status, and once a removal, a sign-out, or "This is not me" shows there, it offers neither, even before the client's `offline` status arrives (#215). The removal calls the existing `account:remove-device` once, so the main process confirms it in its native dialog and revokes the device with `removed`. While it runs, both buttons stay focusable and do nothing (`aria-disabled`, the Project Manager's decision of 2026-11-06), so the dialog gives the focus back to a button; they are not `disabled`. The result shows in the status area. After `removed`, and after `device_revoked`, when the server had revoked the device already and its key was deleted, a fixed sentence says to sign in with GitHub on the Account screen to register this computer again; the header never starts a sign-in. A cancel shows nothing. `pending` says that the removal waits for the server and is tried again at the next start or with "Remove this device" on the Account screen; once the device is no longer `removal_pending`, that message becomes the register-again sentence when the device is `removed`, or `revoked` because the server had revoked it already, as when the header's own removal fails with `device_revoked`, and goes otherwise. A refusal or a failure shows "This device could not be removed." with the account panel's sentence about why. A message stays until the next removal, a GitHub sign-in that starts, or "Reconnect here once" or "Retry now" in the header, which call the removal's `reconnecting` before the reconnect; a connection alone does not end it, so a refused removal stays shown when the device is signed in again, next to `connection_replaced` and its two actions, which come back then (#217).
